Do NOT spend a\\n  `coord:orient` round-trip re-fetching them — you are oriented. Claim your lane\\n  with `coord:declare-intent { intent, items }`, and reach for\\n  `coord:orient` only for what that block deliberately EXCLUDES: the claimable\\n  backlog, full inbox bodies, a mem0 recall. Judge by PRESENCE ONLY — the block is\\n  emitted just when that state CHANGES, and only to hosts that inject it, so its\\n  ABSENCE never means \\\"you are oriented\\\".\\n\\n  **ROUTE B — no such block.** Bootstrap in ONE call:\\n  **`coord:orient { intent }`** — pass your one-line intent for the\\n  task. In a SINGLE round-trip it returns your current assignments\\n  (+ lane + load), the claimable backlog, your unread inbox summary,\\n  the recent plan-events delta, AND a mem0 recall for `intent` — and\\n  it DECLARES `intent` to peers so they see what you're working on.\\n  After a SUCCESSFUL orient, do NOT separately call `coord:plan-events`,\\n  `memory:search`, `coord:inbox`, or `coord:declare-intent` — orient's\\n  result ALREADY contains them (plan-events delta + inbox summary + a mem0\\n  recall) and ALREADY declared your intent; re-calling them just re-pays the\\n  round-trip orient collapsed. Reach for one individually only for a detail\\n  orient deliberately bounds (full inbox bodies, or a mem0 query OTHER than\\n  your intent).\\n\\n  - **Claim your lane.** When the work is plan items, pass them:\\n    `coord:orient { intent, planSlug, planItems: ['P-001', …] }` CLAIMS\\n    those items for you (and releases ones you've moved off). An unclaimed\\n    lane is invisible to the Mug and peers, so work gets double-placed.\\n    (Flipping an item `wip` via `plans:set-status` also auto-claims;\\n    `done` releases. A `claim_conflict` means a live peer holds it —\\n    coordinate with the holder, don't take it.)\\n  - **mem0 (what you know).** orient's recall uses `intent` as the query;\\n    pass `harness` for project-specific facts. Each mem0 hit carries an\\n    `id` — if the user later contradicts something, `memory:forget`\\n    that id. Use `memoryQuery` for a different query, or call\\n    `memory:search` directly. A recall hit is BINDING context, not flavor:\\n    if a recalled fact prescribes a behavior for THIS task, follow it — or\\n    explicitly say why you're not.\\n  - **insights (how things work).** When you hit a confusing failure,\\n    `docs:search` the `agent-insights/` section before grepping code —\\n    many \\\"weird bugs\\\" already have a written runbook.\\n  - **State answers NOW; only the append-only log answers EVER.** `coord:presence` /\\n    `fleet:assignments` are LIVE-STATE — who is alive, who holds what RIGHT NOW; an ended\\n    agent loses its fleet_slug and its presence row is TTL-reaped, so they CANNOT answer an\\n    *ever / was / missed / history* question. Route those to `coord:catch-up { audience }`\\n    (as a member of the fleet/topic) or `coord:feed { audience }` (the firehose) instead —\\n    never a state tool. Full map:\\n    `docs:search` `agent-insights/presence-vs-history-who-is-on-what`.\\n\\nRight layer for READS and WRITES — the memory↔docs boundary:\\n  - **HOW IT WORKS** (a runbook, a failure mode, why-something-is, a hard-won gotcha):\\n    READ via `docs:search` the `agent-insights/` section; WRITE a new/updated MDX under\\n    `apps/operator-docs/src/content/docs/agent-insights/` with a `documents:` frontmatter\\n    anchor to the code it describes. This is the RIGHT place — a doc is code-ANCHORED,\\n    drift-TRACKED (the freshness sweep) and AUTO-HEALED (the doc-steward); a mem0 vector cannot\\n    be anchored/swept/auto-rewritten, so how-it-works left only in mem0 silently rots. Do NOT\\n    park a runbook in mem0.\\n  - **WHY a subsystem/decision is the way it is** (the rationale behind a design, not how it works):\\n    READ via `rationale:feed { topic }` — a topic-keyed projection over the relevant\\n    plan-decisions + work-items + insights, kept fresh by the event engine (`topics:list` for the\\n    topic, then `plans:get`/`docs:get` on an entry's `ref` for the full text). The discoverable\\n    \\\"why\\\" layer — reach for it before grepping plans/decisions by hand.\\n  - **A durable PERSONAL/PREFERENCE fact** (who the user is, a convention, an owner pref):\\n    READ via `memory:search`; WRITE via `memory:remember`. Keep mem0 entries SHORT, and when\\n    the why already lives in a doc/plan store only the non-derivable fact + a POINTER\\n    (`[[<plan-slug>]]`, `/internal/docs/agent-insights/<slug>`), never a copied paragraph.\\n  - **In-flight state** → coord; **derivable session state** (X shipped, migration N, N tests)\\n    → the plan / work-item, never memory.\\nSee the plans `papercusp-su-memory-2026-05-25` and\\n`tool-call-batching-wrappers-2026-06-21`.\\n\\n## Your tool surface is DYNAMIC — the whole 951-tool catalog is reachable on demand\\n\\nYour loaded tool list is usually a small CORE set (the default). Any tool NOT in it is still\\nreachable — you do not need everything loaded up front:\\n- **`tools:find(\\\"<what you want to do>\\\")`** — intent search; matched tools are activated so you can call them directly next.\\n- **`tools:invoke({ name, args })`** — call ANY tool by exact name without loading it (the universal fallback).\\nWhen you need a capability that isn't in your loaded set, SEARCH for it — don't assume it doesn't exist.\\n⚠ Your CLIENT's own tool search (Claude Code ToolSearch, OMP search_tool_bm25) indexes ONLY this\\nadvertised subset — a zero-hit search THERE does NOT mean the tool doesn't exist (EI-9011: agents\\nhit this and degraded to polling). Any tool name you read in an error message, hook text, doc, or\\npeer message is callable RIGHT NOW via `tools:invoke { name, args }`, and `tools:find` searches the\\nFULL server-side catalog regardless of what your client advertises.\\nIf a found tool still errors \\\"not found\\\" when called directly, run it via `tools:invoke` — never guess alternative names.\\n\\nCapability map — the categories that exist (use `tools:find` within any):\\n- **ablation** (1): ablation:report\\n- **access** (4): access:create, access:list, access:pause, access:revoke\\n- **accounts** (16): It printed the usual ERESOLVE 'overriding peer dependency' warnings (react 18 peers under @excalidraw) by 09:27:43Z, then sat silent at ~100% of one core while RSS grew 6.8 -> 7.8 -> 9.7 GB. At kill time (09:54Z) it had written NOTHING: package-lock.json, node_modules/.package-lock.json and operator-core/package.json still had their pre-run mtimes, there were no new top-level node_modules entries and no npm retire dirs. So all 26 minutes were ideal-tree resolution. The added graph is tiny: @linear/sdk plus @graphql-typed-document-node/core, whose graphql peer is satisfied by the root's 16.13.2. The cost is npm re-resolving the whole monorepo's peer graph. Four peers were blocked meanwhile: cf144 EI-25212384048274593, aa2d6 WI-10006164, 25b8a EI-25211687731930615, and 56e2f's verify-tauri-headless launches. test:file readers time out after 180s. Two observability gaps compounded it. (1) npm's logs-max rotation deleted this run's debug log as soon as another npm command started, so the phase was unobservable. (2) install:safe prints no heartbeat or phase while holding the writer. Suggested durable fix: resolution never needs node_modules, so run the resolve (`npm install --package-lock-only ...`) WITHOUT the writer, or in a manifest-only scratch copy as done here with .papercusp/scratch/lin-resolve-tree.mjs (workaround task 0muwi8wrzqmbnhy18rh). Take the writer only for the reify, which with an in-sync lock reads it instead of re-resolving. Also emit a periodic phase/RSS heartbeat while holding the writer.\n\nⓘ Possible STALE RUNTIME, not an open defect: `scripts/npm-install-safe.mjs` (27 newer commits, tip `ef4f15064a7c38c52b6f790474adb31b36e67d1f`) have commits newer than the running build (`d740fc3802`) in `staging`. If this was observed live, verify the deployed generation before triaging; the behavior may already be fixed in the tree.\nFIELD results[].checkpoint\n## Did\nReconciled the stale carry. EI-25218724287547707 is done with committed authority; scheduler assigned this bug. Read the full issue summary and report comment. The original `npm run install:safe -- install --save-dev @linear/sdk@97.0.0 -w @papercusp/operator-core` held `npm-install-79e418157b6a` for 26 minutes, with no manifest/node_modules writes and the child at ~100% CPU/RSS 6.8→9.7 GB. A later add held the same lock long enough for git-sync to time out after 300 seconds. Current source `scripts/npm-install-safe.mjs` runs `spawnSync(\"npm\", npmArgs)` inside `withFsMutex`; `withFsMutex` releases only after its callback settles. Root cause was recorded as workspace fact `root-cause:install-safe-resolution-writer` (id 201536).\n\n## Left\nNo files edited yet. The plan/task list remains active. Manual locks for the two target files were acquired and are being released before compaction.\n\n## Key insight\nResolve package metadata in a unique manifest-only scratch copy under the repo’s shared read lease, serialized by a separate key in the existing fs-mutex. Validate that the source manifests/lock still match before applying resolved files and reifying under the short repo writer. Add asynchronous phase/RSS heartbeats for resolve, reify, and repair.\n\n## Next action\nReacquire manual locks on `scripts/npm-install-safe.mjs` and `scripts/npm-install-safe.test.mjs`; implement the split and regression coverage (resolver: reader present, writer absent; reify: writer present, readers absent; heartbeat phases). GIT_SSH_COMMAND='ssh -i ~/.ssh/custom_ident' npm install git+ssh://git@github.com:npm/cli.git ...     npm install gist:101a11beef ... The `--package-lock-only` argument will only update the `package-lock.json`, instead of checking `node_modules` and downloading dependencies.\nExample:\n    \n    npm install bitbucket:mybitbucketuser/myproject\n\n  * `npm install gitlab:<gitlabname>/<gitlabrepo>[#<commit-ish>]`:\n\nInstall the package at `https://gitlab.com/gitlabname/gitlabrepo` by attempting to clone it using `git`.\n\nIf `#<commit-ish>` is provided, it will be used to clone exactly that commit. If the commit-ish has the format `#semver:<semver>`, `<semver>` can be any valid semver range or exact version, and npm will look for any tags or refs matching that range in the remote repository, much as it would for a registry dependency. If neither `#<commit-ish>` or `#semver:<semver>` is specified, then `master` is used.\n\nAs with regular git dependencies, `dependencies` and `devDependencies` will be installed if the package has a `prepare` script before the package is done installing.\n\nExample:\n    \n    npm install gitlab:mygitlabuser/myproject\n    \n    npm install gitlab:myusr/myproj#semver:^5.0\n\nYou may combine multiple arguments and even multiple types of arguments. For example:\n    \n    npm install sax@\">=0.1.0 <0.2.0\" bench supervisor\n\nThe `--tag` argument will apply to all of the specified install targets. If a tag with the given name exists, the tagged version is preferred over newer versions.\n\nNote: The `--tag` option only affects packages specified on the command line. It does not override version ranges specified in `package.json`. For example, if `package.json` specifies `\"foo\": \"^1.0.0\"` and you run `npm install --tag beta`, npm will still install a version matching `^1.0.0` even if the `beta` tag points to a different version. To install a tagged version, specify the package explicitly: `npm install foo@beta`.\n\nThe `--dry-run` argument will report in the usual way what the install would have done without actually installing anything.\n\nThe `--package-lock-only` argument will only update the `package-lock.json`, instead of checking `node_modules` and downloading dependencies.\n\nThe `-f` or `--force` argument will force npm to fetch remote resources even if a local copy exists on disk.\n    \n    npm install sax --force\n\n## Configuration\n\nSee the `config` help doc. Many of the configuration params have some effect on installation, since that's most of what npm does.\n\nThese are some of the most common options related to installation.\n\n### `save`\n\n  * Default: `true` unless when using `npm update` where it defaults to `false`\n  * Type: Boolean\n\nSave installed packages to a `package.json` file as dependencies.\n\nWhen used with the `npm rm` command, removes the dependency from `package.json`.\n\nWill also prevent writing to `package-lock.json` if set to `false`.\n\n### `save-exact`\n\n  * Default: false\n  * Type: Boolean\n\nDependencies saved to package.json will be configured with an exact version rather than using npm's default semver range operator.\n\n### `global`\n\n  * Default: false\n  * Type: Boolean\n\nOperates in \"global\" mode, so that packages are installed into the `prefix` folder instead of the current working directory. See folders for more on the differences in behavior.\n### `global-style`\n\n  * Default: false\n  * Type: Boolean\n  * DEPRECATED: This option has been deprecated in favor of `--install-strategy=shallow`\n\nOnly install direct dependencies in the top level `node_modules`, but hoist on deeper dependencies. Sets `--install-strategy=shallow`.\n\n### `omit`\n\n  * Default: 'dev' if the `NODE_ENV` environment variable is set to 'production'; otherwise, empty.\n  * Type: \"dev\", \"optional\", or \"peer\" (can be set multiple times)\n\nDependency types to omit from the installation tree on disk.\n\nNote that these dependencies are still resolved and added to the `package-lock.json` or `npm-shrinkwrap.json` file. They are just not physically installed on disk.\n\nIf a package type appears in both the `--include` and `--omit` lists, then it will be included.\n\nIf the resulting omit list includes `'dev'`, then the `NODE_ENV` environment variable will be set to `'production'` for all lifecycle scripts.\n\n### `include`\n\n  * Default:\n  * Type: \"prod\", \"dev\", \"optional\", or \"peer\" (can be set multiple times)\n### `package-lock`\n\n  * Default: true\n  * Type: Boolean\n\nIf set to false, then ignore `package-lock.json` files when installing. This will also prevent writing `package-lock.json` if `save` is true.\n\n### `package-lock-only`\n\n  * Default: false\n  * Type: Boolean\n\nIf set to true, the current operation will only use the `package-lock.json`, ignoring `node_modules`.\n\nFor `update` this means only the `package-lock.json` will be updated, instead of checking `node_modules` and downloading dependencies.\n\nFor `list` this means the output will be based on the tree described by the `package-lock.json`, rather than the contents of `node_modules`.\n\n### `foreground-scripts`\n\n  * Default: `false` unless when using `npm pack` or `npm publish` where it defaults to `true`\n  * Type: Boolean\n\nRun all build scripts (ie, `preinstall`, `install`, and `postinstall`) scripts for installed packages in the foreground process, sharing standard input, output, and error with the main npm process.\n\nNote that this will generally make installs run slower, and be much noisier, but can be useful for debugging.\nChanging this setting will not remove dependencies that are already installed.\n\n`all` allows any url to be installed. `none` prevents any url from being installed. `root` only allows urls defined in your project's package.json to be installed. Also allows url dependencies to be used for other commands like `npm view`\n\n### `allow-scripts`\n\n  * Default: \"\"\n  * Type: String (can be set multiple times)\n\nComma-separated list of packages whose install-time lifecycle scripts (`preinstall`, `install`, `postinstall`, and `prepare` for non-registry dependencies) are allowed to run.\n\nThis setting is intended for one-off and global contexts: `npm exec`, `npx`, and `npm install -g`, where no project `package.json` is involved. For team-wide policy in a project, use the `allowScripts` field in `package.json` (which also supports explicit denials), or configure it in `.npmrc`. Passing `--allow-scripts` on the command line during a project-scoped `npm install`, `ci`, `update`, or `rebuild` is an error.\n\nEach name is matched against a dependency's resolved identity, not against the package's self-reported name. `--ignore-scripts` and `--dangerously-allow-all-scripts` both override this setting.\n\nThis value is not exported to the environment for child processes.\n\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nnpm-ci | npm Docs (https://docs.npmjs.com/cli/v11/commands/npm-ci/)\nciteturn0search1 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: 4 days ago; Select CLI Version:Version 11.21.0 (Legacy) ...   * `npm ci` can only install entire projects at a time: individual dependencies cannot be added with this command. ... NOTE: If you create your `package-lock.json` file by running `npm install` with flags that can affect the shape of your dependency tree, such as `--legacy-peer-deps` or `--install-links`, you must provide the same flags to `npm ci` or you are likely to encounter errors.\n\n# npm-ci\n\nClean install a project\n\nSelect CLI Version:\n\nVersion 11.21.0 (Legacy)\n\n## Synopsis\n    \n    npm ci\n    \n    \n    \n    \n    aliases: clean-install, ic, install-clean, isntall-clean\n\n## Description\n\nThis command is similar to `npm install`, except it's meant to be used in automated environments such as test platforms, continuous integration, and deployment -- or any situation where you want to make sure you're doing a clean install of your dependencies.\n\nThe main differences between using `npm install` and `npm ci` are:\n\n  * The project must have an existing `package-lock.json` or `npm-shrinkwrap.json`.\n  * If dependencies in the package lock do not match those in `package.json`, `npm ci` will exit with an error, instead of updating the package lock.\n  * `npm ci` can only install entire projects at a time: individual dependencies cannot be added with this command.\n  * If a `node_modules` is already present, it will be automatically removed before `npm ci` begins its install.\n  * It will never write to `package.json` or any of the package-locks: installs are essentially frozen.\n\nNOTE: If you create your `package-lock.json` file by running `npm install` with flags that can affect the shape of your dependency tree, such as `--legacy-peer-deps` or `--install-links`, you must provide the same flags to `npm ci` or you are likely to encounter errors. An easy way to do this is to run, for example, `npm config set legacy-peer-deps=true --location=project` and commit the `.npmrc` file to your repo.\n\n## Example\n\nMake sure you have a package-lock and an up-to-date install:\n    \n    $ cd ./my/npm/project\n    \n    $ npm install\n    \n    added 154 packages in 10s\n    \n    $ ls | grep package-lock\n\nRun `npm ci` in that project\n    \n    $ npm ci\n    \n    added 154 packages in 5s\n\nConfigure Travis CI to build using `npm ci` instead of `npm install`:\n    \n    # .travis.yml\n    \n    install:\n    \n    - npm ci\n    \n    # keep the npm cache around to speed up installs\n    \n    cache:\n    \n      directories:\n    \n      - \"$HOME/.npm\"\n\n## Configuration\n\n### `install-strategy`\n\n  * Default: \"hoisted\"\n  * Type: \"hoisted\", \"nested\", \"shallow\", or \"linked\"\n\nSets the strategy for installing packages in node_modules. hoisted (default): Install non-duplicated in top-level, and duplicated as necessary within directory structure. nested: (formerly --legacy-bundling) install in place, no hoisting. shallow (formerly --global-style) only install direct deps at top-level. linked: install in node_modules/.store, link in place, unhoisted.\n\nWe recommend that package authors use `--install-strategy=linked` during development to catch undeclared (\"phantom\") dependencies before publishing: the isolated layout only exposes a package's declared dependencies, so an `import` of a package that was never added to `package.json` can fail instead of resolving by accident and shipping broken. See Catching undeclared (\"phantom\") dependencies.\n\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nnpm-install-test | npm Docs (https://docs.npmjs.com/cli/v11/commands/npm-install-test/)\nciteturn0search2 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: 4 days ago; For `update` this means only the `package-lock.json` will be updated, instead of checking `node_modules` and downloading dependencies. ...             "text": "# AGENTS.md instructions\n\n<INSTRUCTIONS>\n**You are running under Codex CLI.** Use Codex's own native tools for\nplanning, task tracking, and session recovery — here they are the\nstable, correct choice.\n\n> The OMP-native tool list (`todo_write`, `omp:sessions`, `goal`, `job`,\n> `checkpoint`/`rewind`, `search_tool_bm25`, `recipe`, `eval`, `irc`)\n> and the Claude task-panel tools (`TaskCreate`, `TaskList`, …) do not\n> exist in Codex — don't reach for them.\n\n- **Task tracking / planning** — use Codex's native plan tool\n  (`update_plan`, shown as the **Updated plan** panel) to maintain a\n  visible step list for any task with 3+ steps; keep it current as you\n  complete steps.\n- **Edits & shell** — use Codex's built-in `apply_patch` and shell tools\n  rather than inventing scratch files.\n- **Session recovery** — resume prior context with `codex resume`\n  (`--last` for the most recent); sessions live under\n  `$CODEX_HOME/sessions`. There is no `omp:sessions` equivalent to call.\n\nPapercusp coordination is the same on every client: use `coord:*` and\n`locks:*` (not a client-local tool) as the durable cross-session\nauthority for presence, handoffs, escalations, and file claims. For\ncompaction-sensitive work, write a successor brief naming the relevant\nplan/session state before context is summarized.\n\n**Finding a capability:** the ~550-tool catalog shows you only a seed. For a\ntool whose name you don't know, call `tools:find(\"<what you need>\")` (hybrid\nsearch over the full catalog), then call the returned tool directly.\n\nInside Codex `functions.exec`, Papercusp tools may be exposed as normalized\nJavaScript methods. Before calling a direct nested wrapper, check whether that\nexact method is callable, for example\n`typeof tools.mcp__papercusp_su__tools_find === \"function\"`. If it is callable,\nuse `tools.mcp__papercusp_su__tools_find({ query: \"<what you need>\" })` and\n`tools.mcp__papercusp_su__tools_invoke({ name, args })`; `tools.find` and\n`tools.invoke` are not aliases. A missing normalized method is a client exposure\ngap, not evidence the Papercusp capability is absent; use the CLI fallback below.\n\n**Your seeded tools are already callable. Do not re-find them after a restart.**\nThey are in `ALL_TOOLS` as `mcp__papercusp_su__<verb>` (`:`/`-` become `_`).\nCheck `ALL_TOOLS` first, then call the method directly:\n`tools.mcp__papercusp_su__work_items_get({ id, harness })`. Use `tools:find` only for a capability that is\nnot in `ALL_TOOLS`, or for argument keys its type omits; batch several in one query.\n\n**Codex MCP-deferral fallback:** never infer that a Papercusp capability\nis unavailable merely because its MCP schema is absent from the current\nmodel-facing tool list. If the whole `papercusp-su` namespace is missing,\nincluding `tools:find`, or the specific normalized wrapper you need is not\ncallable, use the installed CLI over the same MCP transport. Use `ptool\ntools:find` for discovery and `ptool tools:invoke` when the direct invoke\nwrapper is unavailable; `activated:false` means invoke the returned tool\nthrough `tools:invoke`, not that the tool is missing:\n\n**A direct wrapper returns `stale_tool_contract`?** The MCP session lacks a\n`tools/list` baseline for the current serving generation. Do not retry that\nwrapper or infer the capability is missing. Switch the affected work to the\n`ptool` CLI for this turn: each named `ptool` invocation opens its own MCP\nconnection and lists the current catalog before dispatch. `ptool --list` shows\nnames only; query `ptool tools:find` for an exact schema, then use\n`ptool tools:invoke` or the exact `ptool <group:verb>` call. The CLI uses its\nown connection, so it does not repair the old direct-wrapper session. Return\nto direct wrappers only after that client reconnects and refreshes its list.\n\nWhen you call `exec_command` through `functions.exec`, remember that the outer\nprogram is JavaScript. **Never put a shell command containing `${...}` inside a\nJavaScript template literal**: V8 resolves it before `exec_command` starts, so a\nshell variable such as `${PAPERCUSP_WORKSPACE}` becomes a JavaScript\n`ReferenceError`. Keep that command in a normal JavaScript string, or escape the\ndollar sign as `\\${...}` when a template literal is genuinely necessary.\nThe same boundary bites REGEX metacharacters: every layer between your JS source\nand rg's parser adds one phantom escape (`'^\\\\\\\\{'` reaches rg as `^\\\\\\\\{` and is\nrejected as `repetition quantifier expects a valid decimal`). For LITERAL braces\nuse `rg -n -F '{'` and DROP the anchors (`-F` treats `^` literally too), or keep\nexactly ONE backslash in a single-quoted shell literal (`rg -n -o '^\\{'`).\n\n```bash\nptool_scope=(--workspace=\"${PAPERCUSP_WORKSPACE}\")\nif [[ -n \"${PAPERCUSP_HARNESS_SLUG:-}\" ]]; then\n  ptool_scope+=(--harness=\"${PAPERCUSP_HARNESS_SLUG}\")\nfi\nptool tools:find --json - \"${ptool_scope[@]}\" <<'JSON'\n{\"query\":\"<what you need — or an exact verb name, to read that verb's schema>\"}\nJSON\n```\n\nEach hit carries an **`argSchema`** — the tool's exact accepted keys — not just a\nname. **Read it before any write call, and copy the keys from it.** Guessing\nintuitive argument names is the single most common way a mutation fails here,\nand it costs a retry round trip every time: `locks:acquire` takes `paths` (NOT\n`files`, and it accepts no `harness` — it is workspace-global);\n`improvements:capture` takes `body` (NOT `description` or `evidence`). `ptool\n--list` shows names and descriptions only, so on the `ptool` path it cannot\nanswer this — there, querying `tools:find` for a verb you already know by name\nis the schema lookup.\n\nThen call it with quoting-safe stdin JSON, keys taken from that `argSchema`:\n\n```bash\nptool <group:verb> --json - <<'JSON'\n{...}\nJSON\n```\n\nUse `--json-file` when a payload is easier as a file. Never put arbitrary JSON\nin shell single quotes: one apostrophe breaks the shell before `ptool` runs.\n(`ptool tools:invoke` also works.) `ptool` keeps this session's\n`PAPERCUSP_SID`, so claims and audit attribution stay yours. It is the\nsanctioned escape hatch when Codex defers every MCP tool, not evidence the\nserver or capability is missing.\n\n## An empty `exec_command` result means YIELDED, not failed — pass `yield_time_ms: 30000`\n\n`exec_command` **yields at 10 seconds by default** (`yield_time_ms`, range 250–30000 ms).\nOn yield it returns *whatever has been emitted so far* — for a `ptool` call, which buffers its\nJSON and writes it in one shot at the end, that is **zero bytes** — and hands back a\n`session_id` while your command **keeps running**. Nothing is killed and nothing is lost.\n\n**Pick by how long the call might take — the two remedies have different ceilings:**\n\n- **Under ~30s (the common case): pass `yield_time_ms: 30000`.** One call, no extra steps.\n  Covers `coord:orient`, `plans:get`, `work_items:list`, `release:trace`, `tools:invoke` and\n  every `ptool` invocation above. This is a fix, not a workaround.\n- **Unknown duration, or possibly over 30s: use two-call file capture** (below). `yield_time_ms`\n  is capped at **30000 ms — a hard maximum, not headroom** — so it raises the yield boundary\n  rather than removing it. File capture has **no ceiling**: measured intact at 16.8s / 5,722 bytes,\n  and it does not care how long the command runs.\n\nEither way, if you do get a yield, **poll the `session_id`** — the output is there.\n\n**Read the envelope, never an exit code — a yielded call has NO `exit_code` field:**\n\n| envelope | meaning | what to do |\n|---|---|---|\n| `session_id` present, **`exit_code` absent**, `output: \"\"` | **YIELDED** — still running | poll the `session_id` (`write_stdin`) for the full output |\n| `exit_code` present | genuinely **COMPLETED** | this is a real result |\n\n⛔ **An empty `output` with a `session_id` is not a failure, not an empty dataset, and NOT a\npapercusp tool bug.** Do not retry it, conclude the tool returned nothing, or\nfile it — ~20 agents misread it as a `coord:orient` / `ptool` / `tools:invoke` defect and\nfiled ~35 duplicates. Do NOT spend a\\n  `coord:orient` round-trip re-fetching them — you are oriented. Claim your lane\\n  with `coord:declare-intent { intent, items }`, and reach for\\n  `coord:orient` only for what that block deliberately EXCLUDES: the claimable\\n  backlog, full inbox bodies, a mem0 recall. Judge by PRESENCE ONLY — the block is\\n  emitted just when that state CHANGES, and only to hosts that inject it, so its\\n  ABSENCE never means \\\"you are oriented\\\".\\n\\n  **ROUTE B — no such block.** Bootstrap in ONE call:\\n  **`coord:orient { intent }`** — pass your one-line intent for the\\n  task. In a SINGLE round-trip it returns your current assignments\\n  (+ lane + load), the claimable backlog, your unread inbox summary,\\n  the recent plan-events delta, AND a mem0 recall for `intent` — and\\n  it DECLARES `intent` to peers so they see what you're working on.\\n  After a SUCCESSFUL orient, do NOT separately call `coord:plan-events`,\\n  `memory:search`, `coord:inbox`, or `coord:declare-intent` — orient's\\n  result ALREADY contains them (plan-events delta + inbox summary + a mem0\\n  recall) and ALREADY declared your intent; re-calling them just re-pays the\\n  round-trip orient collapsed. Reach for one individually only for a detail\\n  orient deliberately bounds (full inbox bodies, or a mem0 query OTHER than\\n  your intent).\\n\\n  - **Claim your lane.** When the work is plan items, pass them:\\n    `coord:orient { intent, planSlug, planItems: ['P-001', …] }` CLAIMS\\n    those items for you (and releases ones you've moved off). An unclaimed\\n    lane is invisible to the Mug and peers, so work gets double-placed.\\n    (Flipping an item `wip` via `plans:set-status` also auto-claims;\\n    `done` releases. A `claim_conflict` means a live peer holds it —\\n    coordinate with the holder, don't take it.)\\n  - **mem0 (what you know).** orient's recall uses `intent` as the query;\\n    pass `harness` for project-specific facts. Each mem0 hit carries an\\n    `id` — if the user later contradicts something, `memory:forget`\\n    that id. Use `memoryQuery` for a different query, or call\\n    `memory:search` directly. A recall hit is BINDING context, not flavor:\\n    if a recalled fact prescribes a behavior for THIS task, follow it — or\\n    explicitly say why you're not.\\n  - **insights (how things work).** When you hit a confusing failure,\\n    `docs:search` the `agent-insights/` section before grepping code —\\n    many \\\"weird bugs\\\" already have a written runbook.\\n  - **State answers NOW; only the append-only log answers EVER.** `coord:presence` /\\n    `fleet:assignments` are LIVE-STATE — who is alive, who holds what RIGHT NOW; an ended\\n    agent loses its fleet_slug and its presence row is TTL-reaped, so they CANNOT answer an\\n    *ever / was / missed / history* question. Route those to `coord:catch-up { audience }`\\n    (as a member of the fleet/topic) or `coord:feed { audience }` (the firehose) instead —\\n    never a state tool. Full map:\\n    `docs:search` `agent-insights/presence-vs-history-who-is-on-what`.\\n\\nRight layer for READS and WRITES — the memory↔docs boundary:\\n  - **HOW IT WORKS** (a runbook, a failure mode, why-something-is, a hard-won gotcha):\\n    READ via `docs:search` the `agent-insights/` section; WRITE a new/updated MDX under\\n    `apps/operator-docs/src/content/docs/agent-insights/` with a `documents:` frontmatter\\n    anchor to the code it describes. This is the RIGHT place — a doc is code-ANCHORED,\\n    drift-TRACKED (the freshness sweep) and AUTO-HEALED (the doc-steward); a mem0 vector cannot\\n    be anchored/swept/auto-rewritten, so how-it-works left only in mem0 silently rots. Do NOT\\n    park a runbook in mem0.\\n  - **WHY a subsystem/decision is the way it is** (the rationale behind a design, not how it works):\\n    READ via `rationale:feed { topic }` — a topic-keyed projection over the relevant\\n    plan-decisions + work-items + insights, kept fresh by the event engine (`topics:list` for the\\n    topic, then `plans:get`/`docs:get` on an entry's `ref` for the full text). The discoverable\\n    \\\"why\\\" layer — reach for it before grepping plans/decisions by hand.\\n  - **A durable PERSONAL/PREFERENCE fact** (who the user is, a convention, an owner pref):\\n    READ via `memory:search`; WRITE via `memory:remember`. Keep mem0 entries SHORT, and when\\n    the why already lives in a doc/plan store only the non-derivable fact + a POINTER\\n    (`[[<plan-slug>]]`, `/internal/docs/agent-insights/<slug>`), never a copied paragraph.\\n  - **In-flight state** → coord; **derivable session state** (X shipped, migration N, N tests)\\n    → the plan / work-item, never memory.\\nSee the plans `papercusp-su-memory-2026-05-25` and\\n`tool-call-batching-wrappers-2026-06-21`.\\n\\n## Your tool surface is DYNAMIC — the whole 951-tool catalog is reachable on demand\\n\\nYour loaded tool list is usually a small CORE set (the default). Take refs from a brief's `omission.omittedRefs` (records the budget could only COUNT) or from a clipped record's own `rawRef` — the brief names them precisely so a bounded read is never a dead end. Multiline shell commands need the JavaScript string layer too: use a backtick template literal for `command` when it contains literal newlines, or escape each line break as `\\n`; a literal newline inside a single/double-quoted string causes `compile_error` before bash dispatch.             "text": "Script completed\nWall time 10.5 seconds\nOutput:\n\nFIELD results[].workItem.summary\nObserved 2026-10-06 09:27-09:54Z (su-dab96471, WI-10006364; task 0muwh41dv31bf1n1ozj, lock npm-install-79e418157b6a). `npm run install:safe -- install --save-dev @linear/sdk@97.0.0 -w @papercusp/operator-core` took the writer at 09:27:32Z. It printed the usual ERESOLVE 'overriding peer dependency' warnings (react 18 peers under @excalidraw) by 09:27:43Z, then sat silent at ~100% of one core while RSS grew 6.8 -> 7.8 -> 9.7 GB. At kill time (09:54Z) it had written NOTHING: package-lock.json, node_modules/.package-lock.json and operator-core/package.json still had their pre-run mtimes, there were no new top-level node_modules entries and no npm retire dirs. So all 26 minutes were ideal-tree resolution. The added graph is tiny: @linear/sdk plus @graphql-typed-document-node/core, whose graphql peer is satisfied by the root's 16.13.2. The cost is npm re-resolving the whole monorepo's peer graph. Four peers were blocked meanwhile: cf144 EI-25212384048274593, aa2d6 WI-10006164, 25b8a EI-25211687731930615, and 56e2f's verify-tauri-headless launches. test:file readers time out after 180s. Two observability gaps compounded it. (1) npm's logs-max rotation deleted this run's debug log as soon as another npm command started, so the phase was unobservable. (2) install:safe prints no heartbeat or phase while holding the writer. Suggested durable fix: resolution never needs node_modules, so run the resolve (`npm install --package-lock-only ...`) WITHOUT the writer, or in a manifest-only scratch copy as done here with .papercusp/scratch/lin-resolve-tree.mjs (workaround task 0muwi8wrzqmbnhy18rh). Take the writer only for the reify, which with an in-sync lock reads it instead of re-resolving. Also emit a periodic phase/RSS heartbeat while holding the writer.\n\nⓘ Possible STALE RUNTIME, not an open defect: `scripts/npm-install-safe.mjs` (27 newer commits, tip `ef4f15064a7c38c52b6f790474adb31b36e67d1f`) have commits newer than the running build (`d740fc3802`) in `staging`. If this was observed live, verify the deployed generation before triaging; the behavior may already be fixed in the tree.\nFIELD results[].checkpoint\n## Did\nReconciled the stale carry. EI-25218724287547707 is done with committed authority; scheduler assigned this bug. Read the full issue summary and report comment. The original `npm run install:safe -- install --save-dev @linear/sdk@97.0.0 -w @papercusp/operator-core` held `npm-install-79e418157b6a` for 26 minutes, with no manifest/node_modules writes and the child at ~100% CPU/RSS 6.8→9.7 GB. A later add held the same lock long enough for git-sync to time out after 300 seconds. Current source `scripts/npm-install-safe.mjs` runs `spawnSync(\"npm\", npmArgs)` inside `withFsMutex`; `withFsMutex` releases only after its callback settles. Root cause was recorded as workspace fact `root-cause:install-safe-resolution-writer` (id 201536).\n\n## Left\nNo files edited yet. The plan/task list remains active. Manual locks for the two target files were acquired and are being released before compaction.\n\n## Key insight\nResolve package metadata in a unique manifest-only scratch copy under the repo’s shared read lease, serialized by a separate key in the existing fs-mutex. Validate that the source manifests/lock still match before applying resolved files and reifying under the short repo writer. Add asynchronous phase/RSS heartbeats for resolve, reify, and repair.\n\n## Next action\nReacquire manual locks on `scripts/npm-install-safe.mjs` and `scripts/npm-install-safe.test.mjs`; implement the split and regression coverage (resolver: reader present, writer absent; reify: writer present, readers absent; heartbeat phases).             "text": "Script completed\nWall time 5.9 seconds\nOutput:\n\nnpm-install | npm Docs (https://docs.npmjs.com/cli/v11/commands/npm-install/)\nciteturn0search0 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: 4 days ago; Note: When installing by name without specifying a version or tag, npm prioritizes versions that match the current Node.js version based on the package's `engines` field. ...     GIT_SSH_COMMAND='ssh -i ~/.ssh/custom_ident' npm install git+ssh://git@github.com:npm/cli.git ...     npm install gist:101a11beef ... The `--package-lock-only` argument will only update the `package-lock.json`, instead of checking `node_modules` and downloading dependencies.\nExample:\n    \n    npm install bitbucket:mybitbucketuser/myproject\n\n  * `npm install gitlab:<gitlabname>/<gitlabrepo>[#<commit-ish>]`:\n\nInstall the package at `https://gitlab.com/gitlabname/gitlabrepo` by attempting to clone it using `git`.\n\nIf `#<commit-ish>` is provided, it will be used to clone exactly that commit. If the commit-ish has the format `#semver:<semver>`, `<semver>` can be any valid semver range or exact version, and npm will look for any tags or refs matching that range in the remote repository, much as it would for a registry dependency. If neither `#<commit-ish>` or `#semver:<semver>` is specified, then `master` is used.\n\nAs with regular git dependencies, `dependencies` and `devDependencies` will be installed if the package has a `prepare` script before the package is done installing.\n\nExample:\n    \n    npm install gitlab:mygitlabuser/myproject\n    \n    npm install gitlab:myusr/myproj#semver:^5.0\n\nYou may combine multiple arguments and even multiple types of arguments. For example:\n    \n    npm install sax@\">=0.1.0 <0.2.0\" bench supervisor\n\nThe `--tag` argument will apply to all of the specified install targets. If a tag with the given name exists, the tagged version is preferred over newer versions.\n\nNote: The `--tag` option only affects packages specified on the command line. It does not override version ranges specified in `package.json`. For example, if `package.json` specifies `\"foo\": \"^1.0.0\"` and you run `npm install --tag beta`, npm will still install a version matching `^1.0.0` even if the `beta` tag points to a different version. To install a tagged version, specify the package explicitly: `npm install foo@beta`.\n\nThe `--dry-run` argument will report in the usual way what the install would have done without actually installing anything.\n\nThe `--package-lock-only` argument will only update the `package-lock.json`, instead of checking `node_modules` and downloading dependencies.\n\nThe `-f` or `--force` argument will force npm to fetch remote resources even if a local copy exists on disk.\n    \n    npm install sax --force\n\n## Configuration\n\nSee the `config` help doc. Many of the configuration params have some effect on installation, since that's most of what npm does.\n\nThese are some of the most common options related to installation.\n\n### `save`\n\n  * Default: `true` unless when using `npm update` where it defaults to `false`\n  * Type: Boolean\n\nSave installed packages to a `package.json` file as dependencies.\n\nWhen used with the `npm rm` command, removes the dependency from `package.json`.\n\nWill also prevent writing to `package-lock.json` if set to `false`.\n\n### `save-exact`\n\n  * Default: false\n  * Type: Boolean\n\nDependencies saved to package.json will be configured with an exact version rather than using npm's default semver range operator.\n\n### `global`\n\n  * Default: false\n  * Type: Boolean\n\nOperates in \"global\" mode, so that packages are installed into the `prefix` folder instead of the current working directory. See folders for more on the differences in behavior.\n### `global-style`\n\n  * Default: false\n  * Type: Boolean\n  * DEPRECATED: This option has been deprecated in favor of `--install-strategy=shallow`\n\nOnly install direct dependencies in the top level `node_modules`, but hoist on deeper dependencies. Sets `--install-strategy=shallow`.\n\n### `omit`\n\n  * Default: 'dev' if the `NODE_ENV` environment variable is set to 'production'; otherwise, empty.\n  * Type: \"dev\", \"optional\", or \"peer\" (can be set multiple times)\n\nDependency types to omit from the installation tree on disk.\n\nNote that these dependencies are still resolved and added to the `package-lock.json` or `npm-shrinkwrap.json` file. They are just not physically installed on disk.\n\nIf a package type appears in both the `--include` and `--omit` lists, then it will be included.\n\nIf the resulting omit list includes `'dev'`, then the `NODE_ENV` environment variable will be set to `'production'` for all lifecycle scripts.\n\n### `include`\n\n  * Default:\n  * Type: \"prod\", \"dev\", \"optional\", or \"peer\" (can be set multiple times)\n### `package-lock`\n\n  * Default: true\n  * Type: Boolean\n\nIf set to false, then ignore `package-lock.json` files when installing. This will also prevent writing `package-lock.json` if `save` is true.\n\n### `package-lock-only`\n\n  * Default: false\n  * Type: Boolean\n\nIf set to true, the current operation will only use the `package-lock.json`, ignoring `node_modules`.\n\nFor `update` this means only the `package-lock.json` will be updated, instead of checking `node_modules` and downloading dependencies.\n\nFor `list` this means the output will be based on the tree described by the `package-lock.json`, rather than the contents of `node_modules`.\n\n### `foreground-scripts`\n\n  * Default: `false` unless when using `npm pack` or `npm publish` where it defaults to `true`\n  * Type: Boolean\n\nRun all build scripts (ie, `preinstall`, `install`, and `postinstall`) scripts for installed packages in the foreground process, sharing standard input, output, and error with the main npm process.\n\nNote that this will generally make installs run slower, and be much noisier, but can be useful for debugging.\nChanging this setting will not remove dependencies that are already installed.\n\n`all` allows any url to be installed. `none` prevents any url from being installed. `root` only allows urls defined in your project's package.json to be installed. Also allows url dependencies to be used for other commands like `npm view`\n\n### `allow-scripts`\n\n  * Default: \"\"\n  * Type: String (can be set multiple times)\n\nComma-separated list of packages whose install-time lifecycle scripts (`preinstall`, `install`, `postinstall`, and `prepare` for non-registry dependencies) are allowed to run.\n\nThis setting is intended for one-off and global contexts: `npm exec`, `npx`, and `npm install -g`, where no project `package.json` is involved. For team-wide policy in a project, use the `allowScripts` field in `package.json` (which also supports explicit denials), or configure it in `.npmrc`. Passing `--allow-scripts` on the command line during a project-scoped `npm install`, `ci`, `update`, or `rebuild` is an error.\n\nEach name is matched against a dependency's resolved identity, not against the package's self-reported name. `--ignore-scripts` and `--dangerously-allow-all-scripts` both override this setting.\n\nThis value is not exported to the environment for child processes.\n\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nnpm-ci | npm Docs (https://docs.npmjs.com/cli/v11/commands/npm-ci/)\nciteturn0search1 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: 4 days ago; Select CLI Version:Version 11.21.0 (Legacy) ...   * `npm ci` can only install entire projects at a time: individual dependencies cannot be added with this command. ... NOTE: If you create your `package-lock.json` file by running `npm install` with flags that can affect the shape of your dependency tree, such as `--legacy-peer-deps` or `--install-links`, you must provide the same flags to `npm ci` or you are likely to encounter errors.\n\n# npm-ci\n\nClean install a project\n\nSelect CLI Version:\n\nVersion 11.21.0 (Legacy)\n\n## Synopsis\n    \n    npm ci\n    \n    \n    \n    \n    aliases: clean-install, ic, install-clean, isntall-clean\n\n## Description\n\nThis command is similar to `npm install`, except it's meant to be used in automated environments such as test platforms, continuous integration, and deployment -- or any situation where you want to make sure you're doing a clean install of your dependencies.\n\nThe main differences between using `npm install` and `npm ci` are:\n\n  * The project must have an existing `package-lock.json` or `npm-shrinkwrap.json`.\n  * If dependencies in the package lock do not match those in `package.json`, `npm ci` will exit with an error, instead of updating the package lock.\n  * `npm ci` can only install entire projects at a time: individual dependencies cannot be added with this command.\n  * If a `node_modules` is already present, it will be automatically removed before `npm ci` begins its install.\n  * It will never write to `package.json` or any of the package-locks: installs are essentially frozen.\n\nNOTE: If you create your `package-lock.json` file by running `npm install` with flags that can affect the shape of your dependency tree, such as `--legacy-peer-deps` or `--install-links`, you must provide the same flags to `npm ci` or you are likely to encounter errors. An easy way to do this is to run, for example, `npm config set legacy-peer-deps=true --location=project` and commit the `.npmrc` file to your repo.\n\n## Example\n\nMake sure you have a package-lock and an up-to-date install:\n    \n    $ cd ./my/npm/project\n    \n    $ npm install\n    \n    added 154 packages in 10s\n    \n    $ ls | grep package-lock\n\nRun `npm ci` in that project\n    \n    $ npm ci\n    \n    added 154 packages in 5s\n\nConfigure Travis CI to build using `npm ci` instead of `npm install`:\n    \n    # .travis.yml\n    \n    install:\n    \n    - npm ci\n    \n    # keep the npm cache around to speed up installs\n    \n    cache:\n    \n      directories:\n    \n      - \"$HOME/.npm\"\n\n## Configuration\n\n### `install-strategy`\n\n  * Default: \"hoisted\"\n  * Type: \"hoisted\", \"nested\", \"shallow\", or \"linked\"\n\nSets the strategy for installing packages in node_modules. hoisted (default): Install non-duplicated in top-level, and duplicated as necessary within directory structure. nested: (formerly --legacy-bundling) install in place, no hoisting. shallow (formerly --global-style) only install direct deps at top-level. linked: install in node_modules/.store, link in place, unhoisted.\n\nWe recommend that package authors use `--install-strategy=linked` during development to catch undeclared (\"phantom\") dependencies before publishing: the isolated layout only exposes a package's declared dependencies, so an `import` of a package that was never added to `package.json` can fail instead of resolving by accident and shipping broken. See Catching undeclared (\"phantom\") dependencies.\n\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nnpm-install-test | npm Docs (https://docs.npmjs.com/cli/v11/commands/npm-install-test/)\nciteturn0search2 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: 4 days ago; For `update` this means only the `package-lock.json` will be updated, instead of checking `node_modules` and downloading dependencies. ...
